Sound as a gauge theory and its infrared triangle
Níckolas de Aguiar Alves, André G. S. Landulfo

Abstract
Over the last few decades, there has been a considerable interest on the infrared behavior of various field theories. In particular, the connections between memory effects, asymptotic symmetries, and soft theorems (the “infrared triangle”) have been explored in much depth within the context of high-energy physics. In this paper, we show how sound also admits an infrared triangle. We consider the linear perturbations of the Euler equations for a barotropic and irrotational fluid and show how low-frequency changes in an acoustic source can lead to lasting displacements of fluid particles. We proceed to write these linear perturbations in terms of a two-form potential—a Kalb–Ramond field, in the high-energy physics terminology. This phrases linear sound as a gauge theory and thus allows the use of standard techniques to probe the infrared structure of acoustics. We show how the memory effect relates to asymptotic symmetries in this dual formulation, and comment on how these notions can be connected to soft theorems. This exhibits the first example of an infrared triangle in a condensed matter system and provides new pathways to the experimental detection of memory effects.



I
Introduction

Humankind is interested in physics for many reasons, but it is easy to argue radiation is the most important among them. If we ever saw the stars at night, enjoyed the warmth of the Sun, listened to birds chirping in the woods, or even to binary black holes chirping in other galaxies, it was due to radiation emitted in its various forms.

Astonishing as it is, we still have a lot to learn about radiation. Among the recent lessons, one of the most interesting is the remark that radiative phenomena are not as ephemeral as they may seem. As first noticed by
100
, the passage of a wave pulse can leave lasting effects. Their original setup concerned the emission of gravitational waves by a cluster of stars, and they found the passage of a gravitational wave could leave a permanent displacement between two inertial probes located far away. This was eventually coined the “memory effect” and investigated by several authors
14
;
15
;
23
;
11
;
88
;
8
;
80
;
10
. We now eagerly wait for gravitational wave observatories to test these predictions
41
;
*grant2023ErratumOutlookDetecting
. In the meantime, new memories have been found in in many other theories. For instance, electrodynamics now offers a new route for the experimental observation of memory effects
7
;
89
;
90
;
9
.

While the memory effect is interesting in and of itself, it has been linked to many other concepts that emerge at very large distances or very low energies. For example, the soft theorems in quantum field theories involving massless particles—see, for example, Refs.
32
;
22
;
85
;
27
. These theorems express how a scattering amplitude behaves in the low-energy limit of one of its external legs.
83
showed how the memory effect in general relativity is related to a soft graviton theorem due to
96
. Similarly, the electromagnetic memory effect has been connected to a soft photon theorem
68
.

In addition to this connection, there exists a third related topic in infrared physics, establishing an “infrared triangle.” The soft theorems mentioned above can be understood as dynamical consequences—more specifically, as Ward identities—of an underlying symmetry. Weinberg’s soft graviton theorem, for example, has been shown
84
;
18
to be the expression of invariance under Bondi–Metzner–Sachs transformations
13
;
77
;
78
, which are symmetries of asymptotically flat spacetimes at infinity. See, for example, Ref.
27
for an introduction. Similarly, the soft photon theorem was matched to new symmetries in quantum electrodynamics
43
;
19
. Since these symmetries emerge when considering the long-distance behavior of these theories, they are known as asymptotic symmetries.

The relations between memory, soft theorems, and asymptotic symmetries has led to many directions of investigation. These are often focused on the relations between symmetries and soft theorems, as these subjects are believed to be potential paths to a better understanding of quantum gravity
67
;
69
;
74
;
31
;
70
.

In this paper, we choose to take a different perspective. Memory effects can be understood as a property of the wave equation in four-dimensions—something that can be seen by analyzing the derivation of memory in scalar field theories
90
;
80
. While a massless Klein–Gordon field is usually understood in relativistic terms, it is mathematically described by the same sort of wave equation one could find in nonrelativistic physics. For example, linear perturbations in a fluid—popularly known as “sound”—obey the very same equation. This is arguably the basis for analogue gravity models
92
;
4
. With this remark in mind, one may expect sound to obey a memory effect as well. We show it does. Moreover, one may hope this memory effect is related to asymptotic symmetries and soft theorems, as in more fundamental theories such as general relativity and electrodynamics. We show it is.

Our approach can be summarized as follows. Once the wave equation for acoustic perturbations has been derived—which is done in standard textbooks in acoustics and fluid mechanics
53
;
71
;
87
—one can solve it with a source using a retarded Green function to obtain the memory effect for a scalar field. The introduction of a source is standard when studying the generation of sonic perturbations
53
;
71
;
87
, and the retarded Green function approach is the standard method to derive the linear memory effect in general relativity
15
;
10
;
27
. In this way, a memory effect can be easily derived.

To understand the asymptotic symmetries of the system, we follow the results by
16
;
17
;
35
;
44
concerning asymptotic symmetries for a relativistic scalar field. Namely, we reformulate the problem in terms of a two-form gauge field. The resulting field, known in high-energy physics as the Kalb–Ramond field
50
or
B
-field
72
;
6
;
57
, is one of the simplest examples of
p
-form electrodynamics
45
;
54
. A reformulation of hydrodynamics in terms of a two-form has been considered by other authors—most notably
66
;
86
;
62
—although with much different goals. For our purposes, the key remark is that
p
-form electrodynamics is a gauge theory, and thus its infrared structure can be understood in a now straightforward way. The infrared behavior of
p
-form fields has, in fact, been investigated by several authors
29
;
30
;
1
;
2
;
33
;
36
;
58
;
59
;
75
. This allows a prompt identification of asymptotic symmetries for sound, and then the connection to the previously discussed memory effect.

At last, the infrared triangle is complete once a soft theorem is understood. This is most easily done in the scalar-field formulation. Because the sources in acoustics are akin to the method of images in electrodynamics, the interpretation of a soft theorem is not as obvious as in high-energy theories such as general relativity and electrodynamics. We argue how the previous results by
16
;
17
;
35
can be used to understand acoustic perturbations in terms of soft theorems, but highlight how this should be interpreted differently from the high-energy counterparts.

Some authors have studied individual corners of the “acoustic infrared triangle” before. For example,
26
considered nonlinear flows in Bose–Einstein condensates to propose an analogue model of nonlinear gravitational wave memory.
21
studied soft phonon theorems for a variety of condensed matter systems. Nevertheless, we are not aware of previous investigations of asymptotic symmetries for sound, or of a pursuit for an infrared triangle. In this sense, our results provide the first example of an infrared triangle in a condensed matter system.

The paper is organized in the following way. Section
II
reviews the basic concepts of linear sound we will need in the remaining sections. We first derive the wave equation for acoustic perturbations in the simple case of a homogeneous and quiescent medium. We discuss how sound waves can be generated by time-dependent boundary-conditions, and how these boundary conditions can be traded by localized sources using the method of images commonly employed in electrodynamics. Using the retarded Green’s function for the wave operator, we derive a memory effect predicting the lasting displacement of fluid particles due to changes on the sources.

To proceed with the infrared triangle, it is more convenient to rephrase linear sound in terms of the Kalb–Ramond theory. This is done in Sec.
III
. This rephrasing can be seen as a formulation of analogue gravity models in terms of a two-form. We thus perform the construction using essentially the same hypotheses as in the seminal paper by
92
. Namely, the construction considers an inviscid, irrotational, and barotropic fluid. Nevertheless, it is not necessary to make assumptions about homogeneity or quiescence. We also discuss possible generalizations of this reformulation, how a gauge potential is introduced in the theory and the subtleties of the gauge invariance in this theory—namely, the fact it has gauge-for-gauge symmetries—and how this formulation handles the introduction of the image sources used in the previous discussion of memory in Sec.
II
.

Before proceeding to the discussion of the infrared triangle for acoustics, we review the case of Maxwell electrodynamics in Sec.
IV
. This allows a clear understanding of what our goals in the gauge theory for sound will be, and clarifies the difficulties and strategies to be considered in the following sections. Sections
II
,
III
, and
IV
are mostly independent from each other, but their discussions converge on Sec.
V
. In this section, we rederive the memory effect in acoustics in the two-form formulation by following the same approach used in electrodynamics. The effect is then connected to the asymptotic symmetries for the two-form field in Sec.
VI
. The connection of these asymptotic symmetries to soft theorems had already been clarified in the work of
16
;
17
;
35
. This connection, and the relation between soft theorems and the acoustic memory effect, are briefly considered in Sec.
VII
. The results of the paper are recollected and further discussed in Sec.
VIII
, where we also comment on future prospects. The Appendix briefly reviews some concepts about differential forms and de Rham cohomology that are useful throughout the paper.

Expressions involving differential geometry often employ abstract index notation
94
, and we follow the
{-}{+}{+}{+}
metric signature convention. Lowercase Latin indices from the beginning of the alphabet (
a
,
b
,
c
, …) denote abstract indices in spacetime, while Greek indices denote coordinate indices. Lowercase Latin indices
i
,
j
,
k
, …are occasionally used to denote indices on a three-dimensional manifold, and uppercase Latin indices
A
,
B
,
C
are used to denote indices on the two-sphere. No distinction between abstract and coordinate indices is made in either of these cases. The round metric on the unit two-sphere is given by
\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}
, with Levi-Civita connection
\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}
and Laplacian
\mathcal{D}^{2}=\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}
. Section
IV
, on electrodynamics, is written in Gaussian units with the speed of light set to
c=1
. Sections
V
,
VI
, and
VII
work in units where the fluid background density is
\rho_{0}=1
and the speed of sound is
c=1
.

II
Linear Sound and Acoustic Memory

We begin by considering the linearized theory of sound. We will first derive the wave equation for acoustic perturbations from the basic equations describing ideal fluids in the simplest case of a homogeneous and quiescent background. Then we will argue how one can use the method of images to introduce sources. This discussion is mostly based on Refs.
53
;
71
;
87
. We will end the section by deriving an acoustic memory effect using the retarded Green’s function for the wave equation.

Figure 1:
Left: a moving surface, such as a sphere with oscillating radius, disturbs the fluid around it and generated sound waves. Right: using the method of images, we can understand the sound waves as being generated by “image sources” inside the surface. We solve the problem in a larger region, with the understanding that the interior of the original surface (shaded region) is to be considered unphysical.

II.1
Linear acoustic perturbations and sources

The equations ruling the behavior of an ideal fluid are the mass conservation and Euler equations

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\rho}{t}+\divergence(\rho\vb{v})=0,

(1a)

\displaystyle\rho\quantity[\pdv{\vb{v}}{t} + (\vb{v} \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\vb{v}]=-\gradient P-\rho\gradient\Phi,

(1b)

where
\rho
is the fluid’s mass density,
\mathbf{v}
its velocity,
P
its pressure, and
\Phi
is a potential for an external force. For example,
\Phi
could be the Newtonian gravitational potential.

Our goal is to study linearized perturbations of the fluid flow. Nevertheless, we are not interested only in the free flow, but also in the generation of sound waves. While sound can be produced through various mechanisms, a simple one is to introduce time-dependent boundary conditions when solving the equations describing the fluid flow. For instance, if the skin of a drum is vibrating in a certain known way, this introduces boundary conditions on the airflow close to the drum. The time-dependent boundary conditions create disturbances on the fluid, which propagate as sound waves.

With this in mind, let us consider the example of a pulsating sphere—discussed, for example, in Refs.
71
;
87
. We assume a spherically symmetric fluid flow, with a sphere with oscillating radius put at the origin. This sphere could serve as a simplified model of, for example, the croaking of a frog, or the ringing of a bell. We will assume the time-dependence of the sphere’s radius to be known in advance. Namely, we assume the sphere to have a radius
a(t)
. The fluid is not allowed to cross the surface of the sphere, and thus the velocity of the surface determines the velocity of air. Mathematically, we impose

\mathbf{v}|_{r=a(t)}=\dot{a}(t)\hat{\mathbf{r}},

(2)

where we recognize
\dot{a}(t)
as the radial velocity of the surface of the sphere.

Notice Eq. (
2
) imposes a boundary condition on a moving surface. This makes the problem particularly difficult to analyze. We can, however, try following a different route. Notice that the boundary condition implies, through the divergence theorem,

\displaystyle\int_{r\leq a(t)}\divergence\mathbf{v}\differential{V}

\displaystyle=\oint_{r=a(t)}(\mathbf{v}\dotproduct\hat{\mathbf{r}})r^{2}\differential{\Omega},

(3a)

\displaystyle=\oint\dot{a}(t)a(t)^{2}\differential{\Omega},

(3b)

\displaystyle=4\pi a(t)^{2}\dot{a}(t),

(3c)

\displaystyle=\derivative{t}\quantity(\frac{4\pi a(t)^3}{3}).

(3d)

We can interpret this result in terms of “volume sources” inside the sphere. The continuity equation forbids these sorts of sources, but notice the interior of the sphere is not a physical region. We are not trying to model the fluid flow in there. Therefore, we can understand these sources as “image sources,” in complete analogy to the method of images used in electrostatics. Instead of handling the difficult boundary conditions on a moving surface, we introduce unphysical “image sources” inside the sphere as a trick to simplify our calculations and reproduce the solution outside the sphere. This is illustrated in Fig.
1
. For more details, see, for example, Refs.
53
;
71
.

We thus proceed by manually adding a source term to the equations of motion of the fluid,

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\rho}{t}+\divergence(\rho\vb{v})=\rho q,

(4a)

\displaystyle\rho\quantity[\pdv{\vb{v}}{t} + (\vb{v} \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\vb{v}]=-\gradient P-\rho\gradient\Phi.

(4b)

Above,
q
is a function of time and space modeling (some of the) sources of disturbances in the fluid. We can approximate the case of an pulsating sphere by picking, for example,

q(t,\mathbf{r})=4\pi a(t)^{2}\dot{a}(t)\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{r}).

(5)

This choice reproduces the integral in Eq. (
3
). A more detailed analysis could further develop the choice of source, but this model can already yield good approximations
71
;
38
;
82
.

Since our interest is in describing linearized sound waves, the next step is to consider perturbation theory. For this purpose, we will understand the source terms as generating the perturbations, but not affecting the underlying medium. For the sake of simplicity, we will also assume the background medium to be homogeneous and quiescent, meaning all background quantities are time and space-independent and the equilibrium velocity is zero. To do so, we choose
\Phi=0
. Hence, for any fluid variable
\psi
, we will write

\psi(t,\mathbf{r})=\psi_{0}+\variation\psi(t,\mathbf{r}),

(6)

where
\psi_{0}
is the (constant) equilibrium solution, while
\variation\psi(t,\mathbf{r})
is the perturbation. Since
\mathbf{v}_{0}=\mathbf{0}
, we may write
\mathbf{v}=\variation{\vb{v}}
without the risk of confusion. The linearized equations of motion are then

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\var{\rho}}{t}+\rho_{0}\divergence\mathbf{v}=\rho_{0}q,

(7a)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\partialderivative{\vb{v}}{t}+\gradient\variation{P}=\mathbf{0}.

(7b)

We have two differential equations, but three unknown variables—the latter being the perturbations on velocity, pressure, and density. The missing equation is an equation of state. Hence, we will assume the perturbations to be adiabatic,

\variation{P}=\quantity(\pdv{P}{\rho})_{s}\variation{\rho},

(8)

where the derivative is evaluated at the background solution. The adiabatic speed of sound is then defined as

c^{2}=\quantity(\pdv{P}{\rho})_{s}.

(9)

The equations of motion for the perturbations can thus be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partialderivative{\var{P}}{t}+\rho_{0}\divergence\mathbf{v}=\rho_{0}q,

(10a)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\partialderivative{\vb{v}}{t}+\gradient\variation{P}=\mathbf{0}.

(10b)

Equation (
10b
) implies

\partialderivative{t}\quantity(\curl{\vb{v}})=\mathbf{0}.

(11)

In other words, the vorticity
\curl{\vb{v}}
of the fluid flow is conserved. We will assume it to be initially zero, which automatically implies it is always zero. Therefore, the Helmholtz theorem implies we can obtain a potential function
\phi
such that

\mathbf{v}=\gradient\phi.

(12)

Up to a redefinition of this potential, we see the linearized equations of motion now read

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partialderivative{\var{P}}{t}+\rho_{0}\laplacian\phi=\rho_{0}q,

(13a)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\partialderivative{\phi}{t}+\variation{P}=0.

(13b)

We can differentiate Eq. (
13b
) with respect to time and replace it in Eq. (
13a
). We then find that

-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\partialderivative[2]{\phi}{t}+\laplacian\phi=q.

(14)

We thus have found a wave equation for the potential
\phi
. Once we solve it, we can obtain all other fluid variables through Eqs. (
8
), (
12
), and (
13b
). We recall that the source term
q
was introduced to avoid dealing with complicated boundary conditions. Thus, when solving the wave equation, we will search for a (possibly distributional) solution that holds across all space. This solution may not be physical in some regions of space, precisely due to the boundary conditions we are avoiding for simplicity. In the example of a pulsating sphere, the solution for
\phi
is unphysical inside the sphere.

II.2
Acoustic memory

We are interested only in the sound waves generated by the source term
q
. Therefore, we can solve Eq. (
14
) promptly with the aid of the retarded Green’s function for the wave equation yielding

\phi(t,\mathbf{r})=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\frac{q\quantity(t_{\text{ret}}, \vb{r}')}{\norm{\vb{r} - \vb{r}'}}\differential[3]{r'},

(15)

where the retarded time
t_{\text{ret}}
is given by

t_{\text{ret}}=t-\frac{\norm{\vb{r} - \vb{r}'}}{c}.

(16)

Our original setup considered boundary conditions near the origin, and the image source
q
is meant to model these boundary conditions. Therefore, for any instant
t
, we shall assume
q
to have compact support in a vicinity of the origin and our observation point
\mathbf{r}
to be very far away. Hence, we expand
\norm{\vb{r} - \vb{r}'}
according to

\displaystyle\norm{\vb{r} - \vb{r}'}

\displaystyle=r\norm{\vu{r} - \frac{\vb{r}'}{r}},

(17a)

\displaystyle=r\quantity[1 - \frac{\vb{r}' \vdot\vu{r}}{r} + \order{\frac{1}{r^2}}],

(17b)

\displaystyle=r-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}\dotproduct\hat{\mathbf{r}}+\order{\frac{1}{r}}.

(17c)

By using the above expansion in Eq. (
15
) we find

\phi(t,\mathbf{r})=-\frac{1}{4\pi r}\int q\quantity(t-\frac{r}{c}+\frac{\vb{r}' \vdot\vu{r}}{c}, \vb{r}')\differential[3]{r'}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}},

(18)

where it is implicitly assumed that
\norm{\vb{r}'}\ll r
for all points
\mathbf{r}^{\prime}
in the support of
q(t,\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
. This is completely analogous to radiation-zone calculations in electrodynamics—see, for example, Ref.
95
. We can summarize this result as

\phi(t,\mathbf{r})=-\frac{Q\quantity(t-r/c,\vu{r})}{4\pi r}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}},

(19)

for some function
Q
. Expanding the angular dependence of
Q
in spherical harmonics would lead us to a multipole expansion. For example, the monopole is the spherically symmetric term. Notice that, in acoustics, there is not a symmetry enforcing the conservation of the monopole.

Equations (
12
) and (
13b
) allow us to extract the velocity and pressure from Eq. (
19
) as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(t,\mathbf{r})=\frac{\dot{Q}\quantity(t - r/c, \vu{r})}{4\pi cr}\hat{\mathbf{r}}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}}

(20a)

and

\displaystyle P(t,\mathbf{r})=P_{0}+\frac{\rho_{0}\dot{Q}\quantity(t - r/c, \vu{r})}{4\pi r}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}},

(20b)

respectively.

Let us consider how the position of a fluid particle changes before and after the passage of a sound wave. This is quantified by the Lagrangian displacement
{\bf\it\xi}
. More specifically, if the unperturbed position of a particle is
\mathbf{r}
, then the perturbation takes it to
\mathbf{r}+{\bf\it\xi}(t,\mathbf{r})
. Notice

\partialderivative{\vb*{\xi}}{t}=\mathbf{v}

(21)

at linear order in the perturbations. Between early and late times, the change in the Lagrangian displacement is

\displaystyle\Delta{\bf\it\xi}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{\quantity[\left.Q(\vu{r})\right|_{+\infty} - \left.Q(\vu{r})\right|_{-\infty}]}{4\pi cr}\hat{\mathbf{r}}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}},

(22a)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta\phi\,\hat{\mathbf{r}}}{c}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}}.

(22b)

For
\Delta{\bf\it\xi}(\mathbf{r})
to be well-defined, it is necessary that
\dot{Q}
vanishes at early and late times. It this does not happen, but
\ddot{Q}
vanishes, then we can still see that

\displaystyle\Delta\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{4\pi cr}\quantity[\eval{\dv{Q}{t}}_{+\infty} - \eval{\dv{Q}{t}}_{-\infty}]\hat{\mathbf{r}}+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}}

(23a)

and

\displaystyle\Delta P(\mathbf{r})=\frac{\rho_{0}}{4\pi r}\quantity[\eval{\dv{Q}{t}}_{+\infty} - \eval{\dv{Q}{t}}_{-\infty}]+\order{\frac{1}{r^2}}.

(23b)

In other words, depending on whether
\dot{Q}
or
\ddot{Q}
vanish at early and late times, the Lagrangian displacement, velocity, or pressure present an infrared memory effect, much like the ones predicted in gravity and gauge theories
27
;
7
;
9
;
10
;
11
;
14
;
15
;
23
;
80
;
88
;
89
;
90
;
100
;
8
;
41
. In particular, general relativity also allows a “velocity-coded memory effect”
42
;
10
. Due to the conservation laws of general relativity, gravitational wave memory is associated to changes in the second time-derivative of the mass quadrupole (see, e.g., Refs.
27
;
10
). Dipole variations would violate energy-momentum conservation. Meanwhile, electromagnetic memory is associated to changes in the time-derivative of the electric dipole. Monopole variations would violate charge conservation. Since there are no conservation laws associated with acoustic sources, it follows that monopole variations are sufficient to source memory.

In the example of a pulsating sphere,
Q(t)
is the time-derivative of the sphere’s volume. Hence, a finite
Q
at late times would mean the sphere is eternally expanding at a constant rate of change of volume. In practical experiments, however, it is not necessary to consider the expressions at infinite times—as discussed in Ref.
9
, it should also be possible to observe memory effects at finite times. In this acoustic scenario, it suffices that the sphere continues expanding for a sufficiently long, but finite, time. Here, “sufficiently long” is meant relative to the intermediate, oscillating phase.

III
Sound as a Gauge Theory

Much of the recent theoretical work in memory effects has focused on the connections to asymptotic symmetries and soft theorems in quantum field theory. See, for example, Refs.
27
;
85
for pedagogical introductions. Given the acoustic memory effect derived above, it is natural to search for asymptotic symmetries in the theory of linearized sound. To do so, it is convenient to reformulate the acoustic perturbations in terms of a two-form field. We will thus rely considerably on the theory of differential forms. See Refs.
37
;
40
;
54
;
55
;
65
;
91
, for example, for discussions. In the Appendix we provide a brief summary of the main results we will need.

Our description of sound waves focused on the dynamics of a scalar field. In this sense, we are interested in studying the asymptotic symmetries of a scalar field. This was considered by
16
;
17
;
35
;
44
—see also Ref.
3
for a review. In Refs.
17
;
35
;
44
, it is argued that asymptotic symmetries for scalar fields in four dimensions can be understood by reformulating the theory in terms of a two-form. While we will discuss asymptotic symmetries later on Sec.
VI
, let us now focus on how a two-form can appear when describing acoustics.

We will consider the linearization of the system of equations

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\rho}{t}+\divergence(\rho\vb{v})=0,

(24a)

\displaystyle\rho\quantity[\pdv{\vb{v}}{t} + (\vb{v} \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\vb{v}]=-\gradient P-\rho\gradient\Phi.

(24b)

We further assume that the fluid is irrotational (
\curl{\vb{v}}=\mathbf{0}
) and barotropic (
P=P(\rho)
). In particular, this implies the perturbations to be adiabatic, and we denote the adiabatic speed of sound by
c
as before. Notice
c
may depend on time and space, for we make no further assumptions on homogeneity or quiescence at this stage. Upon linearization, the equations of motion become

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\rho_0}{t}+\divergence(\rho_0 \vb{v}_0)=0,

(25a)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\quantity[\pdv{\vb{v}_0}{t} + (\vb{v}_0 \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\vb{v}_0]=-\gradient P_{0}-\rho_{0}\gradient\Phi,

(25b)

for the unperturbed quantities, and

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\var{\rho}}{t}+\divergence(\var{\rho} \vb{v}_0 + \rho_0 \var{\vb{v}})=0,

(26a)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}\quantity[\pdv{\var{\vb{v}}}{t} + (\var{\vb{v}} \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\vb{v}_0 + (\vb{v}_0 \vdot\vb*{\nabla})\var{\vb{v}}]=\frac{\variation{\rho}\gradient P_{0}-\rho_{0}\gradient\variation{P}}{\rho_{0}}

(26b)

for the perturbed quantities. It is known that the linear acoustic perturbations we are considering can be described in terms of a massless scalar field in a Lorentzian spacetime
92
;
4
. While this is not the route we will follow here, it will be convenient to notice this description employs the “acoustic metric”

\differential{s}^{2}=\frac{\rho_{0}}{c}[-c^{2}\differential{t}^{2}+(\differential{\vb{x} - \vb{v}_0 \dd{t}})^{2}].

(27)

Notice the speed of sound
c
is a function in this expression, not necessarily a constant.

Let us define the three-form
\mathcal{H}
(the “field strength”) by

\mathcal{H}=\variation{\rho}\differential{x}\wedge\differential{y}\wedge\differential{z}\\
-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ijk}(\variation{\rho}v_{0}^{i}+\rho_{0}\variation{v}^{i})\differential{t}\wedge\differential{x^j}\wedge\differential{x^k},

(28)

where
\epsilon_{ijk}
is the three-dimensional volume form in Euclidean space. Notice then that

\differential{\mathcal{H}}=\quantity(\pdv{\var{\rho}}{t} + \div(\var{\rho} \vb{v}_0 + \rho_0 \var{\vb{v}}))\differential{t}\wedge\differential{x}\wedge\differential{y}\wedge\differential{z}.

(29)

Hence,
\mathcal{H}
is a closed form if, and only if, Eq. (
26a
) holds. We will now show that Eq. (
26b
) can be expressed similarly.

In the acoustic spacetime [with metric given by Eq. (
27
)], we can write the Hodge dual of
\mathcal{H}
(see App.
A
) as

{\star}\mathcal{H}=-\quantity(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\var{\vb{v}} + \frac{c^2 \var{\rho}}{\rho_0})\differential{t}+\variation{\vb{v}}\dotproduct\differential{\vb{x}}.

(30)

Because the perturbations are adiabatic, we can also write

{\star}\mathcal{H}=-\quantity(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\var{\vb{v}} + \frac{\var{P}}{\rho_0})\differential{t}+\variation{\vb{v}}\dotproduct\differential{\vb{x}}.

(31)

Due to our assumption that
\curl{\vb{v}}=\mathbf{0}
, it follows that
\curl{\var{\vb{v}}}=\mathbf{0}
. We may thus compute the exterior derivative of
{\star}\mathcal{H}
and find it to be

\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}=-\quantity[\pdv{\var{\vb{v}}}{t} + \grad\qty(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\var{\vb{v}} + \frac{\var{P}}{\rho_0})]\dotproduct\differential{\vb{x}}\wedge\differential{t}.

(32)

Since we assume the flow to be irrotational, it follows from standard vector calculus identities that

\partialderivative{\var{\vb{v}}}{t}+\gradient\quantity(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\var{\vb{v}} + \frac{\var{P}}{\rho_0})\\
=\partialderivative{\var{\vb{v}}}{t}+\quantity(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\grad)\variation{\vb{v}}+\quantity(\var{\vb{v}}\vdot\grad)\mathbf{v}_{0}+\frac{\gradient\variation{P}}{\rho_{0}}-\frac{\variation{P}\gradient\rho_{0}}{\rho_{0}^{2}}.

(33)

By using Eq. (
26b
) we can rewrite the right-hand side of Eq. (
33
) as

\partialderivative{\var{\vb{v}}}{t}+\gradient\quantity(\vb{v}_0 \vdot\var{\vb{v}} + \frac{\var{P}}{\rho_0})=\frac{\variation{\rho}\gradient P_{0}-\variation{P}\gradient\rho_{0}}{\rho_{0}^{2}}.

(34)

The right-hand side vanishes due to the assumption of a barotropic equation of state, which enforces both
\gradient P_{0}=c^{2}\gradient\rho_{0}
and
\variation{P}=c^{2}\variation{\rho}
. Hence, we find

\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}=0.

(35)

We have thus learned that the linear perturbations of an inviscid, irrotational, barotropic fluid flow are described by the equations of motion

\differential\mathcal{H}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}=0,

(36)

where we recall that the “field strength” three-form
\mathcal{H}
is given by Eq. (
28
) and the Hodge dual is meant with respect to the acoustic metric (
27
). This is a dual formulation of the fluid-gravity analogy first proposed by
92
, which eventually developed into the field of analogue gravity
4
. A very interesting property of Eq. (
36
) is the resemblance to Maxwell’s equations in the absence of sources. In fact, the equations we found are precisely the equations of motion for two-form electrodynamics
50
;
45
in the absence of sources—see, e.g., Refs.
54
;
97
for pedagogical introductions.

What could eventual source terms indicate? First, notice that
\differential{\mathcal{H}}=0
was enforced automatically due to the continuity equation. In fact, we could have defined

\mathcal{H}=\rho\differential{x}\wedge\differential{y}\wedge\differential{z}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{ijk}(\rho v^{i})\differential{t}\wedge\differential{x^j}\wedge\differential{x^k}

(37)

and obtained
\differential{\mathcal{H}}=0
as the nonperturbative expression of the mass-continuity equation.
\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}=0
, on the other hand, was obtained through the assumptions that the fluid is irrotational and the flow is barotropic. While this direction will not be pursued here, more general fluid flows involving vorticity or more intricate equations of motion could possibly be described by admitting a nonvanishing
\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}
. Notice this would forbid a simple description in terms of the scalar potential
\phi
, making the differential-form formulation more interesting.

Previous works by
66
;
86
;
62
had considered the idea of describing hydrodynamics in terms of a two-form. One of their main interests was the study of vorticity in fluid flow. Their approach does differ from ours, however, because our focus on linearized perturbations makes the theory naturally formulated on the “acoustic spacetime” introduced by
92
, while
66
;
86
;
62
worked in Minkowski spacetime and included nonlinearities and vorticity in sources to the field equations.

III.1
Gauge potential

The equations of motion for linear acoustics state that
\mathcal{H}
(a three-form) and
{\star}\mathcal{H}
(a one-form) are closed. In general, they do not need to be exact, and thus it may not be possible to obtain a two-form
B
and/or a scalar field
\phi
such that
\differential\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{H}
and
\differential\phi={\star}\mathcal{H}
(and, hence,
\differential\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{H}={\star}\differential\phi
). The conditions for a closed form to be exact is addressed by de Rham cohomology, which is briefly summarized in the Appendix (see Refs.
37
;
40
;
55
;
65
;
91
for various introductions). For our purposes, the main observation is that we are not interested in a theory formulated in a general manifold. Our practical interest is to study the behavior of sound in the radiation zone. This means we want to consider all angles around the source and all possible times, but we are only interested in large radii. For example, we can say we are only interested in what happens for
r>R
, where all image sources are contained inside a sphere of radius
R
. Topologically, this radiation zone is given by
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The factor
\mathbb{S}^{2}
represents all possible angles to which radiation could go, one of the factors of
\mathbb{R}
represents time, and the remaining factor of
\mathbb{R}
is (topologically) the interval
(R,+\infty)
. In
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
—i.e., in the radiation zone—de Rham cohomology ensures that closed three-forms such as
\mathcal{H}
and closed one-forms such as
{\star}\mathcal{H}
are always exact. Hence, in the radiation zone it is always possible to introduce the two-form potential
\mathcal{B}
and the scalar potential
\phi
such that
\differential\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{H}={\star}\differential\phi
.
\phi
is precisely the scalar field we considered in Sec.
II
and that
92
originally considered in analogue gravity. It is the phonon field. Notice that if we had allowed vorticity in the fluid, we would not have
\differential{\star}\mathcal{H}=0
, and it would not be possible to introduce
\phi
. In the language of Sec.
II
,
\curl{\vb{v}}\neq\mathbf{0}
would prevent us from writing
\mathbf{v}=\gradient\phi
. We are now just formulating the same type of statement in the language of differential forms.

To pursue a description of asymptotic symmetries, it will be more convenient to work with
\mathcal{B}
rather than
\phi
. In terms of
\mathcal{B}
, linear acoustics becomes a gauge theory. The potential
\mathcal{B}
is not uniquely defined because we can perform transformations of the form

\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}+\mathcal{F},

(38)

where
\mathcal{F}
is any closed two-form. If
\mathcal{F}
is closed, but not exact, the transformation is a global symmetry. In fact, the existence of a potential that is closed, but not exact, is the origin of the Aharonov–Bohm effect in electrodynamics
79
. If
\mathcal{F}
is exact, then the transformation is a gauge transformation. Gauge transformations that do not vanish at infinity will eventually become the asymptotic symmetries in this description.

At this point, it is natural to ask whether closed
p
-forms in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
are always exact. The answer is negative. There exists precisely one two-form that is closed, but not exact, which is the volume form on the unit sphere (see the Appendix). Since this form is closed, its effects are not captured by the field
\mathcal{H}
, but they can still have physical manifestations. As previously mentioned, this is the same mechanism that gives rise to the Aharonov–Bohm effect in electrodynamics. Our investigations are not topological in nature, so the existence of this field configuration will not impact our results. Nevertheless, the existence of a closed, but nonexact, two-form on the sphere will be relevant for other reasons.

An important subtlety of this theory—and of
p
-form electrodynamics more generally—is that it involves reducible gauge symmetries. Let us consider a gauge transformation for the
\mathcal{B}
-field. We have

\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}+\differential\mathcal{A},

(39)

where
\mathcal{A}
is a one-form. This expression already ensures we are shifting
\mathcal{B}
by an exact form. Since a one-form has four-components at each spacetime point (assuming a four-dimensional spacetime), we may be tricked into believing we can eliminate four components of
\mathcal{B}
. This, however, is not correct. Not all choices of
\mathcal{A}
are valid gauge transformations. If
\mathcal{A}
happens to be exact (
\mathcal{A}=\differential\lambda
), then the alleged gauge transformation would be

\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}+\differential[]\lambda=\mathcal{B}.

(40)

Hence, the gauge transformation becomes trivial. The issue is that
\mathcal{A}
itself has gauge symmetries. In the jargon, there are gauge-for-gauge symmetries, or the gauge symmetry is reducible.
p
-form electrodynamics is, in fact, the textbook example of a theory with reducible gauge symmetry
47
;
46
;
98
;
33
. Notice this does not occur in Maxwell electrodynamics because
0
-forms are only exact if they vanish.

This subtlety means that, to truly fix the gauge of the theory, we also need to fix the gauge of the gauge parameters. As an example, suppose we are interested in Lorenz gauge, which is employed in the analyses by
17
;
58
;
36
, for instance. Noticing the gauge-for-gauge symmetry

\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}+\differential\lambda,

(41)

we choose
\lambda
to satisfy

\differential{\star}\differential\lambda=-\differential{\star}\mathcal{A},

(42)

which is the wave equation for
\lambda
with a source. Solving this equation, we can enforce
\differential{\star}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=0
(and have thus reached Lorenz gauge for
\mathcal{A}
), and are left with a residual gauge-for-gauge symmetry in
\lambda
. Now that
\mathcal{A}
has been gauge-fixed, we notice that

\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}+\differential\mathcal{A},

(43)

and thus choose to solve

\differential{\star}\differential\mathcal{A}=-\differential{\star}\mathcal{B}.

(44)

Given that
\differential{\star}\mathcal{A}=0
from the previous step, we see we are trying to solve Maxwell’s equations with a source current
-\differential{\star}\mathcal{B}
. This source is conserved because
\differential[]=0
. Hence, we can enforce
\differential{\star}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=0
, and thus reach Lorenz gauge for
\mathcal{B}
, with a residual gauge symmetry in
\mathcal{A}
.

The outcome is that we now have the equations

\displaystyle\differential{\star}\differential\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=0,

(45a)

\displaystyle\differential{\star}\differential\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=0,

(45b)

\displaystyle\differential{\star}\differential\lambda

\displaystyle=0,

(45c)

where
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are assumed to be in Lorenz gauge. The first equation is the equation of motion for the theory, while the remaining equations give the conditions on residual gauge symmetry.

Originally,
\mathcal{B}
seemed to have six independent components,
\mathcal{A}
seemed to have four, and
\lambda
had one. Once
\mathcal{A}
was gauge-fixed, it had only three independent components. We used these three components to eliminate three components of
\mathcal{B}
. At this point,
\mathcal{B}
has

6-(4-1)=3

(46)

independent components, where the parentheses compute the number of independent components of
\mathcal{A}
taking gauge-for-gauge symmetry into consideration. Before using residual gauge symmetry, we thus have three components in
\mathcal{B}
, three in
\mathcal{A}
, and one in
\lambda
. The residual freedom in
\lambda
lets us reduce the components of
\mathcal{A}
from three to two. These two components then allow us to reduce the independent components of
\mathcal{B}
from three to one. Hence, the number of degrees of freedom in
\mathcal{B}
is

3-(3-1)=1.

(47)

See also Refs.
46
;
33
for other ways of doing this counting of number of degrees of freedom.

III.2
Image sources

At this stage, one may worry about the source term
q
we introduced in Sec.
II
. Nonperturbatively, such a source term would affect the continuity equation so that
\differential{\mathcal{H}}={\star}q
. Hence,
\mathcal{H}
is no longer closed, which spoils the introduction of the gauge field
\mathcal{B}
. This is not a problem. Recall that the source
q
was introduced in a nonphysical region to model the boundary conditions imposed on the acoustic perturbations. What this source means now is that we are not interested in working with
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as a manifold, but rather with the radiation zone
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, which removes a compact set from the center of space. In the radiation zone, which is the physical region we are interested in,
\mathcal{H}
is a closed form. When trying to extend
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathbb{R}^{4}
we are forced to introduce an image source term.

As an explicit example, let us consider
\mathcal{H}
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, so that
\differential\mathcal{H}={\star}q
. Then we can integrate over a timelike cylinder
\mathcal{V}
defined by all points in spacetime with
r<R
. We find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{V}}{\star}q

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{H},

(48a)

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\oint_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\rho\mathbf{v}\dotproduct\hat{\mathbf{r}}\,R^{2}\sin{\theta}\differential{\theta}\differential{\varphi}\differential{t}.

(48b)

The final integrand obeys the conservation equation away from the source (i.e., it obeys the conservation equation in a region with topology
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
). If we want to write the surface integral as a volume integral, however, we would need to introduce a source term. This would spoil the gauge-theoretic description, but is essentially the method of images we used in Sec.
II
.

IV
Case Study: The Maxwell Infrared Triangle

Before working out the acoustic case in detail, we will first discuss the Maxwell electrodynamics one. This will serve to illustrate most of the techniques we will employ and allow us to set notation and conventions relative to most of the literature. Once electrodynamics is clear, acoustics will follow naturally. Below, we adapt discussions by
7
;
68
;
80
;
9
. We work in Gaussian units with the speed of light set to
c=1
, so the Maxwell equations are written as
63

\differential{\star}\mathcal{F}=4\pi\,{\star}j,

(49)

where
j
is the current one-form and
\mathcal{F}
is the Faraday two-form.

IV.1
The memory effect

The Lorentz force law is given by

\mathchoice{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{b}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{b}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{b}}}}{\nabla^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{b}}}}\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\frac{q}{m}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}\mathchoice{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}},

(50)

where
\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}
is the Faraday tensor,
\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}

\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\lambda)=\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}+\frac{q}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scr (51)

where
\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}\equiv\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}(-\infty)
is the value of the four-velocity at early times.

\mathchoice{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}(\lambda)=\mathchoice{u^{{{0}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{{0}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{{0}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{{0}}}_{{{a}}}}+\frac{q}{mr}\int_{-\infty}^{\lambda}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}\differential{\lambda'}\mathchoice{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}+o\quantity(\frac{1}{r}),

where

This limit must be well-defined for a finite-energy configuration.

, we conclude that

\Delta\mathchoice{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{u^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}=\frac{q}{mr}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}\differential{\lambda'}\mathchoice{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{b}}}_{{{0}}}}+o\quantity(\frac{1}{r}),

where we introduced the shorthand

(55)

Hence, after the passage of an electromagnetic wave, a test charge will undergo a velocity kick. This kick is directly related to a nonvanishing integral of the field-strength tensor. We can then define the “memory tensor”
\mathchoice{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}

and write the Minkowski line element as

\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}

\mathchoice{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}{\gamma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{B}}}}
will be denoted
\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}
. We also write
\mathcal{D}^{2}=\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}
for the Laplacian on the sphere.

We then consider the four-velocity

\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{{0}}}}=\mathchoice{\quantity(\pdv{u})^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\quantity(\pdv{u})^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\quantity(\pdv{u})^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\quantity(\pdv{u})^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}.

(59)

Notice that, in Bondi coordinates, this expression is indeed the four-velocity of a timelike inertial observer. Also notice
\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}{u^{{{a}}}}(\lambda)
will not generically be given by this expression, because it is not in inertial motion—rather, its acceleration is dictated by the Lorentz force law. We conclude then that a sensible definition of “memory” for the electromagnetic field is the tensor

\mathchoice{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{u}}}}\differential{u}.

(60)

We say there is electromagnetic memory whenever
\mathchoice{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{a}}}}
is nonvanishing. This will be physically measurable in terms of
\Delta\mathchoice{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{u^{{{a}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
. Furthermore, notice the memory tensor is defined in a gauge-invariant manner.

It is convenient to express these quantities in the Bondi coordinate system. Earlier, we defined
\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{a}{b}}}}

should be defined in an orthonormal basis. In this case one has, for example,

(62)

Hence,

\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}=\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}.

(63)

With this in mind, we can see the memory tensor only has angular components, because
\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{u}}}}=0
due to antisymmetry and
\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{{(1)}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}=0
because the electromagnetic radiative field is transversal. Hence, we can safely write

\mathchoice{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}{\Delta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}}}}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\ (64)

and know we are accounting for all memory components. Notice then that the memory tensor is a one-form on the sphere. Since we are integrating over
u
and picking a specific coefficient in a large-
r
expansion, the memory tensor does not depend on either
r
or
u
.

We can relate the memory tensor to changes in the Coulombic field and to radiation piercing null infinity. To do so, consider the Maxwell equation

-\mathchoice{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}}}}{\partial^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}}}}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.92825pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.34679pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.67627pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}{r}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt \tensor{\mathcal{F}}{_u_r})-\frac{\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathcal{D}^{{{A}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{A}{u}}}}{\mathcal{F}^{{\mathchoi
